## Page 1


CBARA: Covariate-Balanced-and-Adjusted
Response-Adaptive Randomization
Hengjia Fang∗1 and Wei Ma†1
1Institute of Statistics and Big Data, Renmin University of China, Beijing,
China
Abstract
We propose the covariate-balanced-and-adjusted response-adaptive randomization
(CBARA) procedure for adaptive design in clinical trials, which integrates the comple-
mentary strengths of covariate-adjusted response-adaptive randomization (CARA) and
covariate-adaptive randomization (CAR). The CBARA procedure updates the target
allocation ratio according to observed responses and patient covariate profiles without
requiring a correctly specified model, thereby retaining CARA’s ethical and efficiency
considerations while improving robustness. In addition, the CBARA procedure extends
the CAR principle from fixed target allocation ratios to covariate-adjusted adaptive
target allocation ratios, yet still pursues balance in treatment allocation with respect
to covariate features. This integration is enabled by a newly defined imbalance vec-
tor and three interrelated components: the allocation function, parameter estimation
and update mechanism. We establish the asymptotic properties of covariate imbalance
and the estimators under the CBARA procedure. The results demonstrate that the
CBARA procedure can improve balance for both observed and unobserved covariates
while preserving the consistency of the allocation ratio. The theoretical analysis is
developed through a pseudo-Markov chain framework, where a new discrepancy mea-
sure for transition kernels is introduced to handle the continuity of Poisson equation
solutions with respect to parameters.
Keywords: Adaptive Design, CARA Procedure, Clinical Trial, Covariate Balance, Pseudo-
Markov Chain
∗hengjiafang@ruc.edu.cn
†mawei@ruc.edu.cn

Introduction
1.1
Background
In the context of precision medicine, the design of clinical trials must efficiently exploit
both treatment responses and covariate information, such as biomarkers, in order to im-
prove the allocation mechanism while maintaining a balance between ethical considerations
and inferential efficiency. The covariate-adjusted response-adaptive randomization (CARA)
procedure achieves this objective by dynamically updating treatment allocation probabili-
ties as data accumulate [39, 43]. This approach serves two fundamental purposes. From an
ethical standpoint, it reduces the number of units assigned to treatments that are emerging
4


## Page 5


as inferior for their specific covariate. From an inferential perspective, this design enhances
statistical efficiency by adaptively allocating a larger proportion of units to the treatment
group exhibiting higher response variance. This feature is consistent with the Neyman allo-
cation principle and leads to a reduction in the asymptotic variance of the treatment effect
estimators.
Most existing CARA procedures achieve these two objectives by adjusting the conditional
allocation ratio of units given any covariate value to its theoretical optimum. When the
potential response distribution of each unit conditional on its baseline covariates is known,
one can compute the theoretically optimal allocation probability for each unit according to
a predefined optimization criterion that balances ethical and efficiency considerations. In
practice, however, this distribution is typically unknown. Therefore, before each allocation,
a CARA procedure estimates the optimal allocation probability based on the accumulated
data. The estimate represents the current allocation target and is therefore referred to as
the targeted allocation ratio at the current allocation step.
To achieve the targeted allocation ratio, most of the CARA procedures directly set the
allocation probability to the targeted allocation ratio [5–8, 11, 41, 54, 55]. They can be
analyzed within the framework of Zhang et al. [54]. However, these procedures usually suf-
fer from covariate imbalance across treatment groups. In addition to efficiency and ethics,
a third operating characteristic of adaptive designs is balance, which can also potentially
enhance statistical efficiency [40]. Specifically, under equal allocation, in linear models with
homoscedastic normal errors, achieving marginal covariate balance is equivalent to minimiz-
ing the variance of the estimated treatment effect, thereby improving the statistical power of
hypothesis tests [2, 31, 33, 40]. Therefore, under the CARA procedure, achieving covariate
balance may also improve the efficiency of estimation.
Furthermore, covariate-adaptive randomization (CAR) procedures, also referred to as
covariate-balanced randomization in some literature [4, 34, 49], are primarily designed to
achieve covariate balance across treatment groups under a fixed targeted allocation ratio.
They take the current covariate imbalance into account when determining the allocation
probabilities.
In earlier studies, most CAR procedures focused primarily on balancing discrete covari-
ates [25, 38, 44, 47, 50]. As a generalization of the CAR procedure proposed by Hu and
Hu [25], Zhao et al. proposed a CARA procedure for discrete covariates that balances prog-
nostic covariates [56]. The procedure uses discrete predictive covariates to define strata and
to determine the targeted allocation ratio within each stratum, while balancing prognostic
covariates within strata. Hence, its application is confined to scenarios with discrete covari-
ates, and it only balances these covariates independently within predefined strata. However,
5


## Page 6


theoretical guarantees for its asymptotic properties and inferential validity are not yet avail-
able.
Existing CAR studies have shown that achieving balance in covariates, whether continu-
ous or discrete, improves the efficiency of average treatment effect (ATE) estimation under
linear models [33, 42]. Correspondingly, recent developments in CAR procedures have fo-
cused on achieving marginal balance for continuous covariates [3, 14, 28, 32, 52]. Thus, it is
conceptually possible to achieve marginal balance of continuous covariates within a CARA
procedure. However, without any stratification structure, combining CAR and CARA into
a unified adaptive design still poses significant challenges in both methodology and theory.
1.2
Contributions
In this article, we propose the covariate-balanced-and-adjusted response-adaptive random-
ization (CBARA). It addresses the methodological and theoretical challenges of balancing
continuous covariates under a varying targeted allocation ratio across allocation steps, pro-
viding rigorous theoretical guarantees. As a result of achieving balance, it also improves
estimation efficiency, both theoretically and practically.
For clarity, we assume that the optimal allocation ratio is determined by a finite-dimensional
model parameter that characterizes the joint distribution of covariates and responses. How-
ever, in the CBARA procedure, the targeted allocation ratio is not exactly equal to the
estimated optimal allocation ratio at each step. This is because the sequence of estimates
needs to be smoothed to reduce variation in the targeted allocation ratios across steps. Ac-
cordingly, we refer to the parameter that determines the targeted allocation ratio as the
allocation parameter, which serves as a stabilized proxy for the model parameter estimate.
In Subsubsections 1.2.1–1.2.3, we introduce the three core methodological components of
the CBARA procedure proposed in this paper: covariate imbalance and allocation function,
model parameter estimation, and allocation parameter update mechanism. In Subsubsection
1.2.4, we present the challenges and introduce a new theoretical framework for analyzing the
CBARA procedure. It clarifies why the variation of θn needs to be restricted in Subsubsec-
tion 1.2.3, and motivates the introduction of two allocation parameter update mechanisms
designed to satisfy these restrictions.
1.2.1
Covariate Imbalance and Allocation Function
The CARA procedure proposed by Zhao et al. balances discrete prognostic covariates within
each stratum defined by predictive covariates, while the targeted allocation ratio among units
within the same stratum is equal [56]. When the covariates are continuous, the situation
6


## Page 7


becomes more complex because marginal covariate imbalance is difficult to characterize when
the targeted allocation ratio varies across covariate values and over allocation steps. Even if
covariate imbalance can be characterized, it remains difficult to construct a procedure that
both controls it and prevents severe imbalance in potentially unobserved covariates. The
imbalance of the unobserved covariates has attracted increasing attention in the literature
[29, 30]. Existing studies have shown that, in the context of unequal targeted allocation
ratios, such imbalance may be severe under certain CAR procedures [14, 28]. This may
compromise the consistency of subsequent estimators and the validity of inference.
In this article, we consider only two treatment groups.
If we are only interested in
quantifying the covariate imbalance induced by the randomness between each treatment
assignment and the targeted allocation ratio, we may define the following covariate imbalance

1.2.2
Model Parameter Estimation
Except for stratified designs, the asymptotic properties of parameter estimators in existing
CAR and CARA procedures typically rely on correct model specification. For the CARA
procedure, the estimation approach in [54], based on the maximum likelihood estimator
(MLE), may lack robustness under model misspecification in the CARA procedure. When
the parametric model is misspecified, the oracle model parameter θ∗, defined as the maxi-
mizer of the expected log-likelihood, may not be unique, because the expected log-likelihood
depends on the targeted allocation ratio. This arises from the fact that the log-likelihood may
have a nonzero conditional expectation and vary across covariates and treatment groups, and
the targeted allocation ratio determines the joint distribution of covariates and treatment
groups. The nonzero conditional expectation also violates a key condition of Theorem 2.1
in [54], and consequently, the theory in [54] cannot accommodate model misspecification.
Therefore, it is crucial to develop a universal and robust estimation method for the oracle
model parameters in the CARA procedure that remains valid even when the model is not
correctly specified.
For targeted sequential inference, Chambaz et al. adopted an inverse probability weighted
version of the M-estimator in their design, where the propensity score is taken to be the
allocation probability [10, 11]. This approach provides robustness against model misspecifi-
cation. In CAR procedures and in our CBARA procedure, the allocation probability must
fluctuate around the targeted allocation ratio to maintain covariate balance, and such fluctu-
ations may lead to high variance and instability for this weighted M-estimator. Therefore, in
this context, we use the targeted allocation ratio instead of the actual allocation probability
as the propensity score, since it provides greater stability. Although this breaks the martin-
gale structure of the estimator, under our CBARA procedure it still maintains robustness
and does not rely on correct model specification.
1.2.3
Allocation Parameter Update Mechanism
In this article, the targeted allocation ratio can be parameterized as ρn = ρθn for the al-
location parameter θn. Built upon the theoretical framework in [14], we develop a general
theoretical framework for the CBARA procedure. Although many technical aspects differ,
the theoretical analysis of the CBARA procedure exhibits a similar dependence on the varia-
tion of the allocation parameter sequence {θn} as in [14]. We summarize these dependencies
in the following two assumptions, corresponding to the law of large numbers and the central
limit theorem, respectively.
The weaker assumption on the allocation parameter sequence {θn}, used for establishing

From Assumptions 1.1 and 1.2, it can be seen that the properties of the CBARA procedure
and the model parameter estimation in our article do not rely on the allocation parameter
having a particular asymptotic form, as is required in [1, 54]. They only require that the
changes between successive elements of {θn} remain sufficiently small so that the variation
of {θn} does not substantially disturb the analysis.
However, using the estimate of the model parameter directly as the allocation parameter
may fail to satisfy this variation constraint. To mitigate this issue, we propose two allocation
parameter update mechanisms in Subsection 4.3, which maintain the allocation parameter in
close proximity to the estimate while ensuring controlled, moderate changes at each allocation
step. Therefore, in this article, we distinguish between the estimate of the model parameter
ηn and the allocation parameter θn. These update mechanisms ensure that Assumptions
1.1 and 1.2 are automatically satisfied. Moreover, they further guarantee the stability of
the allocation parameter sequence, even under potential practical irregularities, such as the
presence of response delays or the use of surrogate responses for estimation, as considered in
some adaptive designs [19, 23, 55].

1.2.4
Theory
The CBARA procedure exhibits features of both the CARA procedure and the CAR proce-
dure. Consequently, some properties that are used to analyze the CARA procedure or the
CAR procedure are no longer preserved. For example, the analysis of the CARA procedure
typically relies on the allocation probability having an asymptotic linear representation in
expectation, whereas in the CAR procedure, the dependence of the allocation probability
on imbalance invalidates such a representation [24, 53, 54]. As another example, the CARA
procedure continuously updates the targeted allocation ratio, which destroys the Markovian
property that is commonly used in the analysis of most CAR procedures [22, 25, 28, 32, 52].
In this article, we develop a new theoretical framework for adaptive designs, which allows
the use of tools from Markov chain theory for a class of non-Markovian stochastic processes.
In this setting, even though the stochastic process itself does not possess the Markovian
property, it can still be described using transition kernels. For the CBARA procedure, the
transition kernel corresponding to each step is different, random, and history-dependent.
Accordingly, when the object of analysis is a sum of dependent terms, we apply the Poisson
equation together with a rearrangement of the terms in the sum to decompose each term
into a martingale difference.
This analytical approach may appear similar to that used
for the traditional CAR procedure. However, it is important to note that in the CBARA
procedure, the parameter updates introduce an additional remainder term in each summand,
which captures the disturbance caused by the variation of parameters across successive steps.
Controlling this remainder term so that it does not affect the law of large numbers and the
central limit theorem for the entire sum is a key analytical challenge addressed in this article.
To achieve this, we need to relate the size of the remainder term in each summand to the
magnitude of the corresponding variation in the allocation parameter sequence {θn}. The
size of the remainder term can be decomposed into contributions from the differences in
transition kernels, the corresponding invariant probabilities and allocation functions across
successive steps. Among these differences, the main difficulty lies in the differences of the
transition kernels. Specifically, it is necessary to identify an appropriate discrepancy for the
transition kernels such that, when θ is sufficiently close, the corresponding discrepancy be-
tween the kernels is also sufficiently small. To address this issue, Subsection 5.4 introduces a
new discrepancy between transition kernels. This discrepancy not only captures the variation
in allocation probabilities induced by changes in θ, but also quantifies the change in the dis-
tribution of the imbalance vector increment [(Ti −ρi−1(Xi))ϕ(Xi)] / [ρi−1(Xi)(1 −ρi−1(Xi))].
Using this new discrepancy, we can bound the remainder term in each summand by the cor-
responding variation of {θn}. Therefore, the conditions required to ensure the negligibility of
the remainder term for the law of large numbers and the central limit theorem, respectively,
11


## Page 12


can be summarized as the variation conditions on {θn}, as stated in Assumptions 1.1 and
1.2.
1.3
Organization of the Article
The rest of the article is organized as follows. We first introduce the setup in Section 2, and
then present the CBARA procedure in Section 3. Section 4 develops theoretical results. In
Section 5, we state our proof strategy for the theoretical results. Finally, we conclude our
article and provide directions for future work in Section 6. All technical lemmas, proofs, and
experiments are provided in [15].
2
Framework and Setup
Consider a trial in which patients are sequentially and randomly assigned to two groups.
Let Tn be the treatment assignment of the nth unit, such that Tn = 1 for the treatment and
Tn = 0 for the control. For the nth unit, let Xn ∈Rdx denote the covariate vector used in
the randomization procedure. To evaluate balance between treatment groups, we consider
the balance of the transformed version ϕ(Xn), where ϕ : Rdx →Rd is a prespecified feature
map. Let the potential outcome vector be Yn = (Yn(1), Yn(0)), representing the responses
under treatment and control, respectively.
Denote by Zn an additional covariate vector that is not incorporated into the random-
ization procedure and may even be unobserved by the experimenter. The covariate Zn is
introduced solely for analytical purposes and is not required to be used in the CBARA pro-
cedure. The additional covariate Z may partially overlap with X, be defined as a function of
X and (Y (1), Y (0)), or represent additional covariate information collected after treatment
assignment. We suppose that the vectors {(Xn, Yn, Zn)}n∈N∗are independent and identically
distributed (i.i.d.) random vectors. Let Γ denote the distribution of the covariates Xn, and
let ΓX,Y denote the distribution of (Xn, Yn). Moreover, let ΓX,Y (t) denote the distribution
of (Xn, Yn(t)), for t ∈{0, 1}. In subsequent sections, we will similarly use ΓU to denote the
distribution of an arbitrary random vector U.
Assumption 2.1. The vectors {(Xn, Yn, Zn)}n∈N∗are i.i.d. random vectors.
Before the (n + 1)th allocation, we compute the allocation parameter θn ∈Θ to be used
for this allocation based on all information observed and available up to that point, excluding
any unobserved responses or covariates from future units. Thus, at each step, the allocation
parameter may be updated dynamically based on the information accumulated so far, and
12


## Page 13


in turn influence the allocation of the next unit. Define the σ-field Fn by
Fn = σ (θ0, X1, Y1, Z1, T1, θ1, . . . , Xn, Yn, Zn, Tn, θn) .
We next formally state an assumption that, through the filtration {Fn}n∈N, restricts the
treatment assignment and the allocation parameter update at each step to depend only on
the information collected up to that point, excluding any influence from future units.
Assumption 2.2. The information of the future units {(Xn+k, Yn+k, Zn+k)}k∈N∗is indepen-
dent of Fn for any n ∈N.
Remark 2.1. The most common setting for the allocation parameter is that, θ0 is fixed and
for any n ∈N∗, θn is computed based only on the information and treatment assignments of
the first n units, and the CBARA procedure described in Section 3 satisfies this condition.
In this case,
θn ∈σ(X1, Y1(T1), T1, . . . , Xn, Yn(Tn), Tn).
Moreover, if Tn depends only on the information of the first n units and the treatment
assignments of the first n −1 units, then Assumption 2.2 is automatically satisfied under
Assumption 2.1.
Remark 2.2. The flexible specification of θn allows the framework to accommodate delayed
responses, rare parameter updates, and other practical adaptations in constructing the al-
location parameter sequence {θn}. These capabilities are achieved through the allocation
parameter update mechanism and the model parameter estimation method in Section 3.
Before each allocation, the desirable ratio of treatment assignments for units with covari-
ate value x is specified by the targeted allocation ratio ρ(x). Formally, ρ(x) is a function
of x that represents the desired ratio of assigning such units to the treatment and control
groups, namely ρ(x) : (1 −ρ(x)). For brevity, we refer to “the targeted allocation ratio for
the treatment group” as “the targeted allocation ratio”. The allocation probability specifies
the actual conditional probability that the unit is assigned to the treatment group, given the
past history F and its covariate X.
Under the CBARA procedure in this article, for the (n + 1)th allocation, the targeted
allocation ratio and the allocation probability depend on the current allocation parameter θn.
Accordingly, at the (n+1)th step, the targeted allocation ratio and the allocation probability
can be denoted by ρθn and gθn, respectively. For any θ ∈Θ, ρθ is a function of the covariate
value x alone, while gθ is a function of both x and the imbalance vector defined in (2.1).

Therefore, controlling Λn implies that covariate means weighted by the inverse propensity

In the framework of the CBARA procedure, we consider the data-adaptive allocation
mechanisms such that the allocation of (n + 1)th unit depends on the history Fn and the
information of the unit through the allocation parameter θn, the imbalance vector Λn and

Throughout this article, we always assume that Assumptions 2.1–2.3 hold.
3
General CBARA Procedure
We next provide a detailed description of the CBARA procedure. It consists of three compo-
nents: an allocation function for computing the allocation probabilities of units (see Subsec-
tion 3.1), an estimation method of the model parameter (see Subsection 3.2), and one of two
alternative allocation parameter update mechanisms (see Subsection 3.3). In the absence of
response delay, the CBARA procedure first obtains a sequential estimate ηn of the model
parameter η∗. It then transforms this estimate into the allocation parameter θn. Rather
than ηn, the allocation parameter θn determines both the target allocation ratio ρθn and the
allocation function gθn for the (n + 1)th allocation.
Remark 3.1. In existing CARA literature for continuous covariates, it is typically assumed
that the model parameter is an element of the parameter space Θ that indexes the statistical
model and characterizes the distribution of (X, Y ) [12, 54, 57]. The model is often assumed
to be a generalized linear model. More generally, we define the model parameter η∗as the
unique maximizer of the population-level criterion in (3.2).
If the responses in each treatment group are available without delay, the CBARA proce-
dure proceeds as follows:

In the presence of a delay, the only modification required in the CBARA procedure is in
the estimation of ηn. The estimation can be performed using only the units with observed
responses.
3.1
Allocation Function
The allocation function, which represents the allocation mechanism in the CBARA proce-
dure, is defined by
gθ(Λ, X) = ρθ(X) −

pθ, CΛ and Cθ > 0 are tuning constants. Moreover, we assume that pθ and 1/Cθ are uniformly
bounded away from zero over θ ∈Θ.

Therefore, the allocation function can be interpreted as adjusting the targeted allocation
ratio, where the adjustment magnitude is given by the inner product between the normalized
vector ϕ(X)/[ρθ(X)(1 −ρθ(X))] and the normalized Λ, scaled by ρθ(X)(1 −ρθ(X)) and the
constant pθ. The vector

represents the difference between the increment vectors of Λ under T = 1 and T = 0 at each
allocation. This inner product formulation ensures that each allocation tends to produce an
increment of Λ in a direction opposite to Λ itself. The quantity ρθ(X)(1 −ρθ(X)) represents
the adjustment weight associated with different values of X.
Its inclusion is necessary,
because it influences the asymptotic behavior, which Remark 3.3 will demonstrate. The
constant pθ controls the magnitude of this adjustment uniformly across all X.
Setting
pθ = 1 is already sufficient for any θ. Larger values of pθ are also permissible, provided that
gθ remains within the interval [0, 1]. The constants CΛ and Cθ > 0 are introduced to avoid
division by very small values and to ensure continuity during normalization. The former is
independent of θ.
Remark 3.2. When the targeted allocation ratio ρθ is fixed at a constant value, the CBARA
procedure reduces to a CAR procedure. Under the conditions ρθ ≡ρ for some constant
ρ ∈(0, 1), and ∥ϕ(X)∥≤Cρ(1 −ρ) for some constant C > 0, taking Cθ = C and pθ = 1
yields

C max{∥Λ∥, CΛ}.
Hence, the allocation reduces to a constant targeted allocation ratio ρ plus a bounded linear
adjustment.
Remark 3.3. This allocation function originates from the solution to the shift problem in
the CAR setting. The shift problem is that, with a fixed unequal allocation ratio ρ, the
imbalance of the additional covariate may no longer be centered at 0 [14, 28]. To address
17


## Page 18


this issue, Fang and Ma propose a new form of allocation function [14]
gθ(Λ, x) = ρ + α(x)Tβ(Λ),
where α : Rdx →Rd and β : Rdx →Rd are two functions that satisfy certain conditions
to balance ϕ(X) = X. Similarly, for the CBARA case with a flexible arbitrary targeted
allocation ratio, the allocation function should have the form
gθ(Λ, x) = ρθ(x) + αθ(x)Tβθ(Λ).
The choice of the function αθ affects the asymptotic variance, as shown in Theorems 4.3 and
4.7. The details of how the function αθ influences the variance are provided in the proof of
Lemma F.2 in [15]. The choice of the function βθ affects the boundedness in probability of
the imbalance vector Λn. In light of these considerations, we adopt (3.1) as the allocation
function in our CBARA procedure.

3.3
Allocation Parameter Update Mechanism
Let the oracle allocation parameter be θ∗= η∗. Instead of directly setting θn = ηn, we
consider two mechanisms for updating the allocation parameter θn before the (n + 1)th
allocation, based on the estimate sequence {ηi}i=1:n and the historical allocation parameter
sequence {θi}i=0:(n−1). The initial value θ0 can be chosen arbitrarily, and in particular it may
be chosen such that ρθ0 ≡1/2.

Remark 4.3. The law of large numbers and the central limit theorem rely on different
restrictions on the magnitude of changes between consecutive elements of the allocation pa-
rameter sequence, namely Assumptions 1.1 and 1.2. Once these conditions are satisfied, the
specific values of {θn} do not affect the fundamental asymptotic behavior of the estimators.
In particular, the law of large numbers does not require the convergence of the parameter
sequence, and the asymptotic variance in the central limit theorem depends only on the
limiting value θ∗of the parameter sequence.
Remark 4.4. The asymptotic variance in the central limit theorem admits an explicit an-
alytical form, which corresponds to a linear regression adjustment.
The analytical form
is equivalent to the variance of the imbalance of Z −aTϕ(X) under simple randomiza-
tion with the oracle targeted allocation ratio.
The variable Z −aTϕ(X) is a linearly
adjusted version of Z, and the coefficient vector a depends on the tuning constants of
the CBARA procedure.

5
Proof Strategy
In this section, we outline the proof strategy. A key theoretical contribution of this article
lies in the new discrepancy for transition kernels introduced in Subsection 5.4, which is well
aligned with the setting considered here. Subsections 5.1 and 5.3 present the theoretical
background of the CBARA procedure. In particular, the procedure can be described via
transition kernels, which allows us to employ tools from Markov chain theory. The dis-
crepancy introduced in Subsection 5.4 is then used to handle the disturbance caused by the
variation of {θn}. Subsection 5.2 outlines the approach for establishing that Λ = OP(1), with
some technical details omitted. Subsection 5.5 provides several properties of the allocation
function design, which are used to derive the explicit form of the variance.
5.1
Connection of the CBARA Procedure and Transition Kernels
The transition kernel is commonly used to characterize the dynamics of a Markov chain.
Although the CBARA procedure considered in this article is not Markovian in general, it
can become Markovian when the allocation parameter θn is forcibly fixed at a constant value.
For each θ ∈Θ, define a transition kernel Pθ on the state space X by
Pθ(Λ, h) =
Z
[gθ(Λ, X)h(Λ + ϕ(X)/ρθ(X)) + [1 −gθ(Λ, X)]h(Λ −ϕ(X)/(1 −ρθ(X)))] Γ(dX),
28


## Page 29


for any integrable function h. Then under the forcibly fixed parameter sequence {θn = θ}n∈N,
the stochastic process {Λn}n∈N becomes a Markov chain with transition kernel Pθ. For the
original CBARA procedure, the following lemma establishes its connection with the family
of transition kernels {Pθ}θ∈Θ.
Lemma 5.1. Let the function h : X →R be any integrable function. Then, under the
CBARA procedure, for any n ∈N,
E [h(Λn+1) | Fn] = Pθn(Λn, h).
In this article, we refer to a stochastic process {((Λn, θn), Fn)}n∈N that satisfies the con-
clusions of Lemma 5.1 for any integrable function h as a pseudo-Markov chain. Although
{Λn} is not a Markov chain, we can still employ tools from Markov chain theory, such as the
Poisson equation, as outlined in Subsection 5.3.

5.4.1
Lipschitz Continuity of the Family {Pθ}θ∈Θ with Respect to θ
To characterize the continuity of the transition kernels {Pθ}θ∈Θ, we first define a suitable
discrepancy measure between kernels.
A standard choice is the V -norm, as used in the
adaptive MCMC literature [17, 18]. The V -norm between the transition kernels Pθ and Pθ′
is measured by
sup
Λ
V −1(Λ)∥Pθ(Λ, ·) −Pθ′(Λ, ·)∥V
where V ≥1 is a Lyapunov function and ∥µ∥V := sup|f|≤V |µ(f)| defines a norm on the
measure µ. This discrepancy is also adopted in the framework of the CAR procedure [14].
However, this discrepancy may not be suitable for the CBARA procedure, as we cannot
control the V -norm between the transition kernels Pθ and Pθ′ using d(θ, θ′). The underlying
reason why the V -norm is unsuitable is discussed in detail in Subsubsection 5.4.2.
To address this issue, we develop an alternative way to characterize the discrepancy
between the transition kernels Pθ and Pθ′ using d(θ, θ′).
Definition 5.1 (Coupled Robust Lipschitz Continuity of Transition Kernels). Let P and
Q be transition probability kernels on (X, X). We say that P and Q are (LP, τ, ϵ)-coupled
robustly Lipschitz continuous if there exists a coupling kernel
K : X2 × X ⊗2 →[0, 1]
such that, for all Λ, Λ′ ∈X,
K(Λ, Λ′; A × X) ≤P(Λ, A),
A ∈X,
K(Λ, Λ′; X × B) ≤Q(Λ′, B),
B ∈X,
and the following bounds hold:
0 ≤1 −K(Λ, Λ′; X × X) ≤LPd(Λ, Λ′) + τ,
32


## Page 33


Z
d(u, v)K(Λ, Λ′; du × dv) ≤d(Λ, Λ′) + ϵ.
Definition 5.1 shows that, for any Λ and Λ′, and allowing for a mass deficiency of size
ϵ, the generalized Wasserstein distance between the measures P(Λ, ·) and Q(Λ′, ·) is upper
bounded by LPd(Λ, Λ′) + τ.
In particular, if Λ = Λ′, then the generalized Wasserstein
distance between P(Λ, ·) and Q(Λ, ·) is bounded by τ. This implies that when the mass
deficiency ϵ and the bound τ are zero, we can conclude that P and Q coincide. Therefore,
(τ, ϵ) can be regarded as measuring the discrepancy between the transition kernels P and Q.
Under Assumption 4.2, we establish the following lemma characterizing the coupled ro-
bust Lipschitz continuity of the transition kernels Pθ and Pθ′ in terms of d(θ, θ′).
Lemma 5.2. Suppose that Assumption 4.2 holds and that ϕ(X) has a finite second moment.
Then the family of transition probability kernels {Pθ}θ∈Θ is robustly Lipschitz continuous
with a Lipschitz constant LP ≥0, that is, for any parameters θ, θ′ ∈Θ, the kernels Pθ and
Pθ′ are (LP, LPd(θ, θ′), LPd(θ, θ′))-coupled robustly Lipschitz continuous.
The discrepancy result in Lemma 5.2 can be interpreted as the Lipschitz continuity of
the family {Pθ}θ∈Θ with respect to θ in the sense of Definition 5.1, and the non-expansivity
of the transition kernels Pθ with respect to the state variable Λ. This result can be extended
to the family of n-step transition kernels {P n
θ }θ∈Θ via the following corollary.
Corollary 5.1. Let {Pθ}θ∈Θ be a family of transition probability kernels that is robustly
Lipschitz continuous with a Lipschitz constant LP ≥0. Then for any θ, θ′ ∈Θ and any n ∈
N, the probability kernels P n
θ and P n
θ′ are (nLP, nLPd(θ, θ′) +
n(n−1)L2
P
2
d(θ, θ′), nLPd(θ, θ′))-
coupled robustly Lipschitz continuous.
Therefore, when the family {Pθ}θ∈Θ is robustly Lipschitz continuous, the family {P n
θ }θ∈Θ
is also Lipschitz continuous, with a Lipschitz constant inflated at a rate of n2.
5.4.2
Limitations of the V -Norm for the CBARA Procedure
To understand why the V -norm works in the context of the CAR procedure but not in the
CBARA procedure, consider how the transition kernel Pθ changes with respect to θ under the
CAR and CBARA procedures. More specifically, we examine the change in the imbalance
vector Λ when a unit with covariate value x is assigned to the treatment group.
Under the CAR procedure, when the imbalance vector Λ encounters a unit with covariate
x and this unit is assigned to the treatment group, the transition corresponds to moving from
Λ to Λ+(1−ρ)ϕ(x) with probability P(X = x)gθ(Λ, x). In this case, changing θ only affects
the allocation probability gθ(Λ, x), while the destination state remains unchanged. Thus,
33


## Page 34


the V -norm discrepancy between the corresponding transition kernels can be controlled by
the difference between the allocation probabilities, and the V -norm is suitable for the CAR
procedure.
In contrast, under the CBARA procedure, when the imbalance vector Λ encounters a unit
with covariate x and this unit is assigned to the treatment group, the transition corresponds
to moving from Λ to Λ+ϕ(x)/ρθ(x) with probability P(X = x)gθ(Λ, x). Consequently, chang-
ing θ not only alters the probability but also changes the destination state itself. Therefore,
when the V -norm is used to measure the discrepancy between the transition kernels, the
change in the destination state induced by θ under the CBARA procedure can lead to a
non-negligible discrepancy between Pθ(Λ, ·) and Pθ′(Λ, ·). In particular, even when θ and
θ′ are close, the corresponding transitions may place probability mass on different states,
namely Λ + ϕ(x)/ρθ(x) and Λ + ϕ(x)/ρθ′(x). As a result, the quantity ∥Pθ(Λ, ·) −Pθ′(Λ, ·)∥V
may remain bounded away from zero, since the two measures are supported on different
locations in the state space. Unfortunately, the V -norm is insensitive to the geometry of the
state space and may assign maximal discrepancy to probability measures with nearby but
non-identical supports. These observations suggest that the V -norm used in the adaptive
MCMC literature is suitable for the CAR procedure but may not be well suited for analyzing
the CBARA procedure.
5.4.3
Results for the New Discrepancy Measure
Let the family of functions {hθ}θ∈Θ possess certain α-H¨older continuity for any α ∈(0, 1)
within any sufficiently small region in both Λ and θ. With continuity properties on {hθ}θ∈Θ
and {Pθ}θ∈Θ, together with some properties of Pθ, it can be shown that the difference
|(P n
θ hθ)(Λ) −(P n
θ′hθ′)(Λ)| can be controlled by a multiple of (n2 + 1) [d(θ, θ′)]α for any

By applying the martingale central limit theorem and establishing a law of large numbers for

Therefore, it suffices to analyze the asymptotic

For central limit theorems considered in this article, the corresponding function hθ takes
the form
hθ(Λ) = EX∼Γ [(gθ(Λ, X) −ρθ(X)) fθ(X)] ,
(5.3)
for some function fθ depending on the specific setting. It can be shown that the associated
solution ˆhθ to the Poisson equation admits an explicit expression, up to an unknown additive
constant. Therefore, the asymptotic variance can be computed explicitly. It is worth noting
that not all functions hθ used in the proof admit the special form in (5.3). For instance,
when applying the martingale central limit theorem, we need to establish a law of large
numbers for the conditional variance, and the corresponding function hθ associated with
the conditional variance does not take the form in (5.3). It is therefore necessary to use
the analysis developed in Subsection 5.4 to establish the continuity of the Poisson equation
solution ˆhθ, and thereby prove the negligibility of the remaining term.
35


## Page 36


6
Discussion
In this article, we propose the CBARA procedure. It combines the advantages of the CARA
and CAR procedures, allowing for adjustment of the targeted allocation ratio while simul-
taneously balancing covariates. We show that it improves both the balance of additional
covariates and the performance of weighted M-estimation.
Valid Inference
Building on these theoretical properties, we can further conduct statis-
tical inference. For the IPW estimator

Since the theorem provides an explicit expression for the variance, it is feasible to conduct
inference by constructing a consistent estimator of the variance. Moreover, inference can also
be conducted using other estimators.
Estimation Using Machine Learning
In this article, the targeted allocation ratio is up-
dated by estimating a finite-dimensional parameter via weighted M-estimation. In practice, a
promising extension is to incorporate machine learning methods to learn an optimal targeted
allocation ratio in a data-driven manner, potentially improving flexibility and performance
in complex settings.
36


## Page 37


Multiple Treatment Arms
The current CBARA procedure focuses on the case of two
treatment groups. It can be naturally extended to settings with multiple treatment arms,
where the allocation mechanism and the corresponding theoretical analysis can also be
adapted to the multi-arm setting.

## Page 39


where ∥· ∥denotes the corresponding norm in each space.
A.2
Structure of the Proofs
The CBARA procedure considered in this article is not Markovian in general. However, if
the allocation parameter θ is fixed, the stochastic process {Λn} becomes a Markov chain. We
denote the corresponding transition kernel by Pθ, which depends on the allocation parameter
θ. This simplified scenario is relevant to our CBARA procedure. As shown in Lemma D.1,
for any n ∈N, the conditional expectation at the (n + 1)th step of the CBARA procedure,
given the history, can be expressed in terms of the transition kernel Pθn and the imbalance
vector Λn. In this article, we refer to a stochastic process that satisfies the conclusions of
Lemma D.1 in Subsection D.1 as a pseudo-Markov chain.
The works [17, 18] inspired our approach to analyzing a pseudo-Markov chain associated
with the family of transition kernels {Pθ}, by leveraging both the properties of Pθ and
transition kernels. The properties required for this analysis are those specified in Assumption
H.1. Lemma D.8 summarizes all lemmas in Subsections D.2 and D.3 and is used to verify
Assumption H.1. In Subsections H.1–H.3, building on Assumption H.1, we establish bounds
and continuity properties of Pθ and certain related functions with respect to different values
of θ. Building on this, Lemma H.6 in Subsection H.4 establishes a weak law of large numbers
(WLLN) in a straightforward manner.
The main text presents numerous results concerning asymptotic normality. A key re-
quirement for establishing asymptotic normality is that variations in the parameter do not
affect the limiting distribution. This asymptotic normality result is formalized in Lemma
G.1 in Subsection G.1. The proof of Lemma G.1 relies on bounds and continuity properties
of certain functions associated with the central limit theorem. By applying the theorems and
corollaries in Subsections H.1–H.3, we establish these properties in Subsection G.2. More-
over, the center and the variance of the asymptotic distribution presented in the main text
correspond to the case when the parameter is fixed at its limiting value θ∗, under which the
procedure becomes Markovian. The specific values of the center and variance under this
fixed parameter require Lemmas F.1 and F.2 in Section F, and the proofs of these lemmas
rely heavily on the form of the allocation function (3.1).
At this point, the majority of the technical work has been completed, and Section E
applies the above law of large numbers and the central limit theorem results to the model
parameter estimation. Consequently, the theorems presented in the main text are proved in
Sections B and C.

D
Lemmas for the CBARA Procedure
D.1
Definition of the Transition Kernel
Let the state space be X = Rd and the parameter space be Θ. For each θ ∈Θ, define a
transition kernel Pθ on X by
Pθ(Λ, h) =
Z
[gθ(Λ, X)h(Λ + ϕ(X)/ρθ(X)) + [1 −gθ(Λ, X)]h(Λ −ϕ(X)/(1 −ρθ(X)))] Γ(dX).
(D.1)
In this subsection, we show, via the following lemma, that the evolution of the process {Λn}
under the CBARA procedure can be fully characterized by the transition kernel Pθ defined
above.
Lemma D.1 (Lemma 5.1). Let the function h : X →R be any integrable function. Then,
for any n ∈N,
E [h(Λn+1) | Fn] = Pθn(Λn, h).
Proof. By definition of Λn+1, we have
E [h(Λn+1) | Fn]
= E [h(Λn + (Tn+1 −ρθn(Xn+1))ϕ(Xn+1)/[ρθn(Xn+1)(1 −ρθn(Xn+1))]) | Fn]
= E [E [h(Λn + (Tn+1 −ρθn(Xn+1))ϕ(Xn+1)/[ρθn(Xn+1)(1 −ρθn(Xn+1))]) | Fn, Xn+1] | Fn]
= E [[h(Λn + ϕ(Xn+1)/ρθn(Xn+1))gθn(Λn, Xn+1)
+h(Λn −ϕ(Xn+1)/(1 −ρθn(Xn+1)))(1 −gθn(Λn, Xn+1))] | Fn]
= EX∼Γ [h(Λn + ϕ(X)/ρθn(X))gθn(Λn, X) + h(Λn −ϕ(X)/(1 −ρθn(X)))(1 −gθn(Λn, X))]
= Pθn(Λn, h),

where the third equality is from Assumption 2.3 and the fourth equality is from the inde-
pendence between Xn+1 and Fn in Assumption 2.2.
Although many of the subsequent proofs involve not a fixed function h but a family of
functions {hθ}θ∈Θ, Lemma D.1 still implies that
E [hθn(Λn+1) | Fn] = Pθn(Λn, hθn),
since hθn is Fn-measurable.
D.2

In this subsection, based on Assumption 4.2, for the transition kernels (D.1), we establish
a particular form of continuity (Definition H.3). The key step is provided by the following
lemma.
Lemma D.2 (Lemma 5.2). Suppose that Assumption 4.2 holds and that ϕ(X) has a fi-

Lipschitz continuous with a Lipschitz constant LP ≥0.

Lemma D.6. Under Assumptions 4.1 and 4.4, for any R > 0, there exists some dR ∈N∗
and δR,P > 0 such that for any θ ∈Θ,
P dR
θ (Λ, ·) ≥δR,Pµleb,B(Λ,R).
49


## Page 50


The proof of Lemma D.6 is presented in Subsection D.6. The argument is somewhat
technical and relies on three main observations.
First, Assumption 2.3 implies that the
transition kernel Pθ controls a constant multiple of a random walk. Second, Assumption
4.4 shows that this random walk, starting from any point, admits a density on an open ball
with a possibly nonzero center after 2s steps. Therefore, using the increment distribution
of the random walk, the density on this open ball can be concentrated into a mass at the
center. As n increases, after n steps, the region where the original random walk and the
concentrated random walk admit densities grows on the order of n. Third, as in the original
random walk, the concentrated random walk starting from the original point has zero mean
and is therefore concentrated around that point. As a result, its typical range grows on the
order of O(√n), which is much slower than n. Therefore, when n is sufficiently large, the
open ball on which the original random walk admits a density necessarily covers the original
point. Finally, by the translation invariance of the random walk, this conclusion extends to
any starting point.

If V is replaced by V α for some α ∈(0, 1], the corresponding constant is denoted by Lα.
The proof of Lemma D.7 is presented in Subsection D.7. The proof follows as a simple
corollary of Lemma H.7.
Lemma D.8. Let the Lyapunov function V (Λ) = eλ1∥Λ∥be as defined in Lemma D.4. If
Assumptions 4.1, 4.2, 4.3 and 4.4 hold, then Assumption H.1 holds.
Proof. Item 1 of Assumption H.1 can be deduced from Lemma D.7. Item 2 of Assumption
H.1 can be deduced from Lemma D.4. Item 3 of Assumption H.1 can be deduced from
Lemma D.2. Item 4 of Assumption H.1 can be deduced from the definition of the Lyapunov
function V (Λ) = eλ1∥Λ∥.

Central Limit Theorem
In this section, we extend our analysis to a more general setting that relaxes the independence
condition of Assumption 2.1.
We now consider a scenario where the distribution of the
additional covariate is parameterized by θ, and the parameter θ is sequentially updated as
87


## Page 88


the procedure progresses.
Specifically, the joint conditional distribution of (Xn, Zn) given the past information Fn−1
is denoted by ΓX,Z,θn−1, which depends on a parameter θn−1 that can be determined by the
history. A crucial constraint in our model is that the conditional marginal distribution of Xn
remains a fixed distribution Γ, regardless of the parameter θn−1. This more general setting
allows the definition of Zn to depend on the targeted allocation ratio ρθn−1.
Let Zn = (Z(c)
n , Z(u)
n ) be a two-dimensional vector. For a given parameter θ and a random
variable Z, we define the conditional mean function fZ,θ as
fZ,θ(x) := Eθ[Z|X = x].
This function represents the expected value of Z given Xn = x, under the model parameter-
ized by θ.

H
Lemmas for the Markov Chain
In this section, suppose that the parameter space Θ is a complete metric space with the
metric d(θ, θ′) and the state space X is a Polish (complete separable metric) space with the
metric d(Λ, Λ′).
H.1
Definitions and Lemmas on Transition Kernels
H.1.1
Definitions of Transition Kernels
In this section, we formally introduce the definitions and assumptions regarding the continu-
ity and stability of the transition kernel family {Pθ}θ∈Θ. These definitions and assumptions
are crucial for the subsequent analysis. We begin by defining the necessary metrics and
continuity properties.
Definition H.1 (Robust Wasserstein Metric). Let ν and ν′ be two finite measures. The
robust Wasserstein metric between them is defined as:
Wd,δ(ν, ν′)
:= inf {Wd (µ, µ′) | ∥µ∥= ∥µ′∥, ∥ν −µ∥≤δ, ∥ν′ −µ′∥≤δ, ν ≥µ, ν′ ≥µ′} ,
where Wd denotes the standard d-Wasserstein distance.
Definition H.2 (Coupled Robust Lipschitz Continuity of Transition Kernels, Definition
5.2). Let P and Q be transition probability kernels on (X, X). We say that P and Q are
(LP, τ, ϵ)-coupled robustly Lipschitz continuous if there exists a coupling kernel
K : X2 × X ⊗2 →[0, 1]
such that, for all Λ, Λ′ ∈X,
K(Λ, Λ′; A × X) ≤P(Λ, A),
A ∈X,
K(Λ, Λ′; X × B) ≤Q(Λ′, B),
B ∈X,
and the following bounds hold:
0 ≤1 −K(Λ, Λ′; X × X) ≤LPd(Λ, Λ′) + τ,
Z
d(u, v)K(Λ, Λ′; du × dv) ≤d(Λ, Λ′) + ϵ.
104


## Page 105


Definition H.3 (Robust Lipschitz Continuity of a Family of Transition Kernels). A fam-
ily of transition probability kernels {Pθ}θ∈Θ is robustly Lipschitz continuous with a Lip-
schitz constant LP ≥0 if, for any parameters θ, θ′ ∈Θ, the kernels Pθ and Pθ′ are
(LP, LPd(θ, θ′), LPd(θ, θ′))-coupled robustly Lipschitz continuous.
H.1.2
Lemmas on Transition Kernels

Lemma H.3. Suppose that P and Q are (LP, τ, ϵ)-coupled robustly Lipschitz continuous.
Then, for any Λ, Λ′ ∈X, the robust Wasserstein metric between the probability measures
P(Λ, ·) and Q(Λ′, ·) satisfies
Wd,LP d(Λ,Λ′)+τ (P(Λ, ·), Q(Λ′, ·)) ≤d(Λ, Λ′) + ϵ.
Proof. This follows directly from the definitions of (LP, τ, ϵ)-coupled robustly Lipschitz con-
tinuity and the robust Wasserstein metric. In particular, the coupling kernel K in Definition
107


## Page 108


H.2 guarantees the existence of a coupling π = K(Λ, Λ′; ·) such that the expected distance
under π satisfies
E(x,y)∼π[d(x, y)] ≤d(Λ, Λ′) + ϵ
and
1 −π(X × X) ≤LPd(Λ, Λ′) + τ.
Denote the marginal measure of π by µ and µ′. Then we have µ(·) ≤P(Λ, ·), µ′(·) ≤Q(Λ′, ·),
and
Wd,LP d(Λ,Λ′)+τ ≤Wd (µ, µ′) ≤E(x,y)∼π[d(x, y)] ≤d(Λ, Λ′) + ϵ.
This completes the proof.
Theorem H.1. Suppose that P and Q are (LP, τ, ϵ)-coupled robustly Lipschitz continuous.

K : X2 × X ⊗2 →[0, 1]

Kn(Λ, Λ′; A × X) ≤P n(Λ, A),

H.2
Definitions and Assumptions on Functions
H.2.1
Definitions on Functions
Definition H.4 (Local H¨older Continuity). Let h : X →Rm be a function. We say that h
is (∆, ψ, α)-locally H¨older continuous if for any Λ, Λ′ ∈X with d(Λ, Λ′) < ∆, it holds that
∥h(Λ) −h(Λ′)∥≤ψ(Λ)[d(Λ, Λ′)]α,
where ∆> 0, α ∈(0, 1], and ψ : X →(0, ∞) is a nonnegative function depending on Λ.

H.3

H.3.1
Assumptions
Definition H.5 (Joint Local H¨older Continuity). A family of functions {hθ}θ∈Θ is said
to be ((∆, ψ, α), ( ˜∆, ˜ψ, ˜α))-jointly locally H¨older continuous if it satisfies the following two
conditions:
1. (Continuity in state) For each θ ∈Θ, the function hθ(·) is (∆, ψ, α)-locally H¨older
continuous. That is, for some ∆> 0, α ∈(0, 1], and a function ψ : X →(0, ∞),
∥hθ(Λ) −hθ(Λ′)∥≤ψ(Λ)[d(Λ, Λ′)]α
for all Λ, Λ′ with d(Λ, Λ′) < ∆.
2. (Continuity in parameter) For each state Λ ∈X, the mapping θ 7→hθ(Λ) is
( ˜∆, ˜ψ, ˜α)-locally H¨older continuous. That is, for some ˜∆> 0, ˜α ∈(0, 1], and a function
˜ψ : X →(0, ∞),
∥hθ(Λ) −hθ′(Λ)∥≤˜ψ(Λ)[d(θ, θ′)]˜α
for all θ, θ′ with d(θ, θ′) < ˜∆.
Assumption H.1 (Simultaneous Stability, Ergodicity, and Regularity). The family of tran-
sition kernels {Pθ}θ∈Θ and the associated Lyapunov function V : X →[1, ∞) are assumed to
satisfy the following conditions:
1. (Simultaneous Geometric Ergodicity) For each θ ∈Θ, the transition kernel Pθ
is positive Harris recurrent with a unique invariant probability πθ. Furthermore, for
any a ∈(0, 1], there exists a constant La > 1, independent of θ, such that for all n ≥0
and Λ ∈X:
∥P n
θ (Λ, ·) −πθ∥V a ≤La(1 −L−1
a )nV a(Λ).
2. (Simultaneous Drift Condition) For any a ∈(0, 1], there exist constants βa ∈
(0, 1) and ba < ∞, independent of θ, such that for all Λ ∈X:
PθV a(Λ) ≤βaV a(Λ) + ba.
113

3. (Robust Lipschitz Continuity of the Kernel) The family of transition kernels
{Pθ}θ∈Θ is robustly Lipschitz continuous with a constant LP ≥0 that holds uniformly
for all θ ∈Θ.
4. (Regularity of the Lyapunov Function) There exists a finite constant Lln V > 1
such that

In this subsection, we present the simulation results of several randomization procedures.
Consider the case where the covariate vector X is 3-dimensional. To construct a covariate
vector that is both correlated and bounded, we proceed as follows:
1. Generate a random vector from a multivariate normal distribution from a correlated
multivariate normal distribution with covariance matrix

3. Transform the second component x to max{min{x, 2}, −2}/2 to ensure boundedness.
4. Transform the third component monotonically to have a uniform distribution on [−1, 1].
The working model used for estimation is
Y (T) = T(α1 + X1γ1) + (1 −T)(α0 + X1γ0) + X2β2 + X3β3 + ϵ,

Moreover, the feature map ϕ used in the covariate
imbalance and in (3.1) is defined as ϕ : x 7→(1, xT)T. Thus, we are concerned with the
balance of the treatment group sizes and the first moments of the covariate vector X. The
randomization procedures in this subsection are implemented with the following components:
1. When the number of allocated units n ≤20, the allocation probability is fixed at 0.5.
When n exceeds the threshold 20, the allocation probability is set equal to the targeted
allocation ratio itself (direct) or determined by the allocation function (3.1) (balance).

Note that larger responses are considered preferable and by (J.1), the
maximum maxx ρθ(x) equals 0.5 (CRD), plogistic(|α1 −α0| + |γ1 −γ0|) (logistic), and
probit(|α1 −α0| + |γ1 −γ0|) (probit). When the first setting of the targeted allocation
ratio is used, the CBARA procedure reduces to CAR or simple randomization, whereas
the latter two correspond to the classical targeted allocation ratio settings within the
CARA framework [5, 54, 57].
The primary performance measures considered in this simulation include
1. the magnitude of the imbalance vector,
2. the convergence rate of the imbalance of the additional covariate,
3. and the mean squared error (MSE) of the ATE estimator.

Y (1) = 4.5 + 4.7X1 −0.6X2 −0.6X3,
Y (0) = 7.5 + 1.7X1 + 2.9X2 + 1.4X3.
For each case, the sample sizes are n = 200, 800, and we conduct the simulation for N = 2000
times. The simulation results are given in Tables 1 and 2.
Table 1 indicates that, under the last two targeted allocation ratio settings, the aver-
age response is higher than under CRD. Across all sample sizes, model specifications, and
estimation methods, the average responses under direct allocation and the balance alloca-
tion mechanism are nearly identical. This indicates that the balance allocation mechanism
achieves improved covariate balance without sacrificing the average response. Furthermore,
under the balance allocation mechanism, the norm of the primary covariate imbalance vector
∥Λn∥= OP(1), whereas under direct allocation it grows at rate OP(√n). The imbalance
of the additional covariate, ∥Ψn∥, grows at OP(√n) under both allocation mechanisms, but
is reduced under the balance allocation mechanism. These results indicate that applying
the balance allocation mechanism significantly improves the balance of X and also partially
reduces imbalance for the additional covariate.
Table 2 shows that the logistic and probit targeted allocation ratios under the CBARA
procedure exhibit similar variability. The choice of estimation method does not materially
affect the overall variations of the allocation ratios across allocation steps, although weighted
methods are theoretically more robust. Moreover, improved covariate balance leads to lower
mean squared errors for the IPW estimators of the ATE. As the sample size increases, the
efficiency gains of the balance allocation mechanism become more pronounced. This corre-
sponds to the fact that ∥Λn∥= OP(1) under the balance mechanism, which provides a clear
advantage compared with the direct allocation mechanism in relatively large samples. Over-
all, the balance allocation mechanism improves covariate balance and substantially enhances
the accuracy of ATE estimators.
136
